--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Before switch time: score += 1 per stationary robot → robots should MOVE
- After switch time: score += 1 per moving robot → robots should STOP
- Switch cue: a GREEN wall lights up (color 2 in ARGoS? Let me check - clr options are {1,2,3,4,5,6})
- The green wall is the signal to stop moving

**Key insight:** Robots need to:
1. Move before the green signal appears
2. Stop after the green signal appears
3. Green = color perceived by camera (C=7 condition with color for green)

Wait, looking at the color IDs: the problem says the wall lights up GREEN. In ARGoS color conventions typically: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. So green = color 2.

**Champion analysis:** Score 12827.34 with a complex 4-state machine. The champion has:
- State 0: Repulsion → transitions to state 1 when neighbors < 6
- State 1: Repulsion → multiple transitions
- State 2: Exploration → transitions including perceiving color 6 (cyan)
- State 3: Stop → transitions on white floor or many neighbors

The champion seems somewhat effective but doesn't clearly implement the move-then-stop strategy. A simpler approach might be: Explore until green is perceived, then Stop.

**Ideal strategy:**
- State 0: Exploration (move randomly) - active before green appears
- State 1: Stop - triggered when green wall is seen (C=7, color=2)
- With transition from Stop back to Explore if green disappears? No, once green appears it stays.

**My candidates:**

1. **Champion copy** (elitism)
2. **Simple 2-state**: Explore → Stop when green (C=7, l=2)
3. **3-state with repulsion then stop**: Repulsion phase, then stop on green
4. **Exploitation of champion**: Tweak parameters slightly
5. Various explorations

Let me design 14 machines:

1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Exploit champion - tweak rep gains and thresholds
3. Exploit champion - tweak transition probabilities
4. Simple: Explore then Stop on green (2 states)
5. Simple: Stop only (like rank 2 but pure stop)
6. 2-state: Move → Stop on green signal
7. 3-state: Explore → Stop on green, with fallback
8. 3-state: Repulsion → Stop on green
9. 4-state: More complex move+stop with green detection
10. Exploit rank 3 FSM
11. 2-state attraction then stop
12. 3-state with GoToColor green then stop
13. Exploit champion variation 2
14. Exploration: different topology

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations:**
- Champion (score 11719.19): Simple 2-state machine - Explore with rwm=30, transitions to Stop when green (color 2) seen with p=0.95, or with tiny random probability (p=0.002). Stop state has tiny probability of going back to explore.
- The green wall signal (color 2) is the key cue to switch from moving to stopping.
- LED colors: only {0,4,5,6} are valid (not 1,2,3!). Green is NOT a valid LED color.

**Strategy:**
- The champion is elegant: move until green is detected, then stop. The tiny p=0.002 random transition ensures robots occasionally stop even before green (maybe helping a little before switch time? Actually no - before switch time robots should MOVE). Wait - before switch, moving is good. After switch, stopping is good. So the champion's approach of moving until green is seen, then stopping, is exactly right.
- The small p=0.002 random transition to Stop from Explore is slightly wasteful before the switch but keeps it robust.

**Exploitation of champion:**
- Try different rwm values (faster/slower turning)
- Try different detection probabilities (p0x0)
- Try p0x1 = 0.0 (never randomly stop before green) or very tiny
- Try 3-state variants with intermediate states

**Exploration:**
- 3-state: Explore → Stop on green, with a repulsion intermediate
- 2-state with attraction instead of exploration
- Variants with neighbor-count transitions added

Note: cle values must be in {0,4,5,6}.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations:**
- Champion (10727.21): 2-state, Attraction (att=2) → Stop. Trigger: see green (color 2, p=0.95) or tiny random (p=0.002). Stop has tiny random back to Attraction.
- Attraction (moving toward neighbors) is better than pure Exploration for the moving phase - makes sense as robots cluster and move, ensuring they're counted as "moving" before the switch.
- Simple 2-state structure dominates. 3-state machines underperform.
- The key parameters to tune: att gain, detection probability for green, rwm if using exploration.

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Vary att gain around 2.0 (try 1.0, 1.5, 2.5, 3.0, 4.0, 5.0)
- Vary green detection probability (0.9, 0.95, 0.99, 1.0)
- Vary random stop probability (0.001, 0.002, 0.005, 0.0)
- Try repulsion instead of attraction (keeps robots moving)
- Try no random transition (p0x1 = 0 isn't valid, use very tiny)
- Try combining attraction + exploration in 3-state

**Important**: LED colors must be in {0,4,5,6}. No color 2 for LEDs.

The champion's logic: Attraction keeps robots moving before green appears. When green is seen, transition to Stop. The tiny random ensures eventual stopping as fallback.

Let me generate 14 machines:

1. Champion (exact copy)
2. att=1.5, otherwise same
3. att=2.5, otherwise same
4. att=3.0, otherwise same
5. att=1.0, otherwise same

7. att=2.0, p=0.9 for green detection
8. att=2.0, p=1.0 for green detection (always stop when green seen)
9. att=2.0, p0x1=0.001 (less random stopping)
10. att=2.0, p0x1=0.005 (more random stopping)
11. Repulsion instead, stop on green
12. 3-state: Attraction → Stop on green, with attraction fallback from Stop

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations:**
- Champion (10067.29): att=4.0, p_green=0.95, p_random=0.002, Stop has tiny p=0.001 back
- Trend: higher attraction gain is better (att=4 > att=3 > att=2)
- Repulsion also works well (score 10630)
- Simple 2-state dominates; 3-state machines underperform
- p=0.99 for green detection is WORSE than 0.95 - interesting, maybe slightly lower probability handles noise better
- Adding extra transitions (3 from state 0) hurts

**Why att=4 works:** Strong attraction keeps robots moving actively (clustered movement = definitely moving), and when green wall appears, they stop. The tiny random p=0.002 ensures some robots stop even if they don't see green directly.

**Exploitation:**
- Try att=5.0 (maximum)
- Try att=4.5 (between 4 and 5)
- Try att=4 with different p_green (0.85, 0.9, 0.95)
- Try att=4 with different p_random (0.001, 0.003)
- Try repulsion with higher gains (rep=3, 4, 5)
- Try rep=2 with different parameters

**Exploration:**
- Try GoToColor green (behavior 8, clr=2) - move toward green wall when it appears, then stop? No wait, GoToColor moves TOWARD the color, which would be good after switch (moving = bad). Actually we want to STOP when green appears.
- Try a hybrid: attraction + stop on green, with also a repulsion intermediate
- Try att=4 with p0x1 removed (only green trigger)

Let me be careful: att max is 5.0, so try att=5.

## Reasoning

**Key observations:**
- Champion (8905.78): att=5 (maximum!), p_green=0.95, p_random=0.002, Stop→Attract with p=0.001
- att=5 is the maximum allowed and it's the best - strong attraction keeps all robots moving together
- The tiny random stop transition (p=0.002) is crucial - removing it (worst performer #2) caused catastrophic failure
- Adding extra transitions from Stop (worst #3) also hurts - keep Stop simple
- p_green around 0.85-0.95 all perform similarly; 0.95 is good
- Repulsion around 5 might also be worth trying

**Why att=5 wins:** Maximum attraction means robots cluster tightly and move together constantly before the switch. When green appears, they stop. The tiny p=0.002 provides a fallback for robots that can't see the green wall.

**Now at maximum att=5, what else can we tune?**
- p_green: try 0.7, 0.8, 0.85, 0.9, 0.95, 1.0
- p_random_stop (p0x1): try 0.001, 0.002, 0.003, 0.005
- p_return_to_attract (p1x0): try 0.0005, 0.001, 0.002
- Repulsion at max (rep=5) instead of attraction
- Try rep=5 with different probabilities
- Try combining att=5 with neighbor threshold condition added
- Try GoToColor (behavior 8) toward green as the "stop" trigger doesn't make sense... wait, GoToColor moves TOWARD green which is bad after switch. Keep attraction.

**Important insight:** The random p=0.002 from state 0 to Stop before seeing green costs some score before switch (robots stop randomly). But it's essential for fallback. Maybe reducing it slightly (0.001) could help?

Actually the worst performer had p0x1=0.002 with only 1 transition (no random), score 14652. So the random IS needed. Let's keep it around 0.001-0.003.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations:**
- Champion (8510.80): att=5, p_green=0.95, p_random_stop=0.002, p_return=0.0005 (5e-04)
- The breakthrough was p_return=0.0005 (half of 0.001) - once stopped, rarely go back to attraction
- p_random_stop=0.002 is important (0.001 slightly worse, 0.005 worse)
- p_green=0.95 is good; lower values (0.8-0.9) slightly worse but close
- p_return=0.002 is BAD (too much returning to movement after switch)
- p_return=0.001 is also worse than 0.0005

**Trend:** Minimizing the return probability from Stop→Attract is key. Once robots stop (after green), they should almost never return to moving.

**Exploitation:**
- Try p_return even smaller: 0.0001, 0.0002, 0.00025, 0.0003
- Try p_random_stop variations: 0.001, 0.0015, 0.002, 0.003
- Try p_green variations: 0.95, 0.9, 0.85 with p_return=0.0005
- Try p_return=0 effectively (very tiny like 1e-5 or 0.0001)

**Exploration:**
- Try 3-state: Attract → Stop (on green), with a third state that also stops (cascade)
- Try att=5 but with neighbor-count transition added
- Try repulsion with p_return=0.0005

**Key insight:** p_return=0.0005 means after switching to Stop, only 0.05% chance per step of returning. Over 600 steps after switch that's ~26% chance of returning once. Lower = better. Try 0.0001 (10% chance over 1000 steps).

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations:**
- Champion (8194.38): p_return=1e-04 (0.0001) - even smaller is better!
- Clear trend: lower p_return → better score (0.0001 > 0.0002 > 0.0003 > 0.0005 > 0.001)
- p_random_stop=0.002 and p_green=0.95 are consistent winners
- 3-state machines and repulsion are clearly worse
- The minimum meaningful probability is approaching zero

**What to try for p_return:**
- 0.0001 is current best - try even smaller: 5e-05, 2e-05, 1e-05
- But there's a floor: if p_return=0 robots can never leave Stop even if they entered it before the switch (though with p_random=0.002 that's unlikely to matter much before switch)
- Actually: before switch, if a robot randomly enters Stop (p=0.002/step), it stays stuck. With p_return=0.0001, expected time in Stop = 10000 steps. That's way longer than the run. So lower p_return means any pre-switch stops are permanent (costly before switch). But apparently this is outweighed by post-switch gains.

Wait - actually the random stop p=0.002 means ~0.2% per step. Before switch (~500 steps), probability of entering Stop = 1-(0.998)^500 ≈ 63%. So most robots stop randomly before the switch! But with p_return very small they stay stopped... This seems bad.

Hmm, but the scores are improving. Maybe the green wall detection is catching most robots quickly after switch, and the pre-switch random stops are just the cost of the mechanism.

**Let me try:**
- Even smaller p_return: 5e-05, 2e-05, 1e-05, 0 (but 0 might not be valid - use 1e-10 or minimum)
- Smaller p_random_stop: 0.001, 0.0015 with small p_return
- Different p_green with small p_return
- Try p_random_stop even smaller (0.0005) to reduce pre-switch stops

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations:**
- Champion (8171.71): p_random_stop=0.0015, p_return=1e-04
- p_return=0 also works well (score 8267.69) - once stopped, never return
- p_green can range 0.75-0.95 with similar results; lower detection prob is fine
- The key parameters that matter: p_random_stop (0.001-0.002 range), p_return (0-0.0001 range)
- p_return=0 is valid and nearly as good as 1e-04

**Insight:** p_return=0 means robots that enter Stop state never leave it. Before switch, if a robot randomly stops (p=0.0015/step), it stays stopped permanently - costs before switch. But after switch, all robots that see green stop permanently. The tradeoff is: lower p_random_stop = fewer pre-switch accidental stops, but less fallback for robots that can't see green after switch.

**What to tune now:**
- p_random_stop: try 0.001, 0.0012, 0.0015, 0.0018, 0.002 with p_return=0 or 1e-04
- p_return: try 0 (already good), 5e-05, 1e-04
- p_green: try 0.7, 0.75, 0.8, 0.85, 0.9, 0.95
- Also: maybe having NO random stop transition (only green detection) could work if p_green is high enough, but we saw removing it was catastrophic earlier

**Combinations to try:**
1. p_random=0.0015, p_return=0 (combining best random stop with zero return)
2. p_random=0.001, p_return=1e-04
3. p_random=0.001, p_return=0
4. p_random=0.0012, p_return=1e-04
5. p_random=0.0018, p_return=1e-04
6. p_green=0.7 variations
7. p_green=0.6 - maybe even lower detection is better?

## Reasoning

**Key observations from round 9:**
- Champion (7648.92): p_random_stop=0.0015, p_return=0.0 (never return to attraction after stopping)
- p_return=0 is confirmed best (permanent stop once triggered)
- p_random_stop=0.0012 with p_return=1e-04 also very good (7698.34)
- p_green=0.95 seems best for champion, but 0.75, 0.85, 0.7 also competitive
- p_random_stop=0.001 is WORSE than 0.0015 - so we need it a bit higher
- p_green=0.002 is terrible (too low detection)

**Parameter sweet spot:**
- p_random_stop: 0.0012-0.0018 range (0.001 too low, 0.002 OK)
- p_return: 0.0 or very close to 0
- p_green: 0.7-0.95 all work well

**Final round strategy - exploit the champion heavily:**
- Try p_random_stop: 0.0013, 0.0014, 0.0015, 0.0016, 0.0017
- Try p_return: 0.0 (confirmed best)
- Try p_green: 0.5, 0.6, 0.65, 0.7, 0.75, 0.8, 0.85, 0.9, 0.95 with p_return=0
- Also try p_random_stop=0.0012 with p_return=0 (combining best of #1 and #2)

The winning formula: att=5, p_green=0.95, p_random=0.0015, p_return=0. Let me squeeze out every last improvement.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 27 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.69 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
